DECLARATION
--- seg-736fc2a74f04299375cee488 [needs-review] ---
For the purposes of verification in the 2023 Cultural Heritage ICMS Program, exercise 2025, we declare that this documentary set, composed of the documents listed below, which form part of the "QII-C Register Dossier for the asset 'The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity'", contains genuine information based on historical, iconographic, photographic, cartographic, oral interview, and original production of the asset's documentation carried out by qualified technical personnel, as described in the Technical Sheet.

As well as administrative process documentation consistent with Quadro II-C of Portaria IEPHA n. 06, of March 31, 2023, Portaria IEPHA n. 26, of November 25, 2023, and Normative Deliberation n. 01/2023.

Because it is true, we sign this present.

1. Introduction
--- seg-81c3d7eba5cb54d59290d316 [needs-review] ---
Missing a Disc
Carlos Drummond de Andrade
Love,
I am sad because
I am the only living Brazilian
who has never seen a UFO.
On my street, everyone saw
and spoke with its crew
in a mixed language of Carioca
and green luminescent signals
that anyone understands, right?
They boarded (as guests)
flew around
over there, beyond
without a passport
and a negative tax declaration,
without dollars, love, without dollars.
They returned full of news
and superiority.
They look at me with benevolent contempt.
I am the outcast,
the one who only sees a truck
cinema poster, street hole
and other pedestrian evidence.
A friend I have
every week goes to see his UFO
on Itaipu beach.
This one doesn't say anything to me,
out loud, but the way,
the eyes! They tell of prodigies
turned simple by their weekly nature
secret only to those who are
unable to listen and understand a UFO.
Why is the UFO refused to me,
only to me?
Perhaps so that the acronym
is not entirely lost, for finally
nothing is more identified
than a UFO present today
in São Paulo, Bahia
Barra da Tijuca and Barra Mansa.
(The shepherds of this village
make fun of me
because I search, in vain I search
Municipality of Varginha | Mayor: Verdi Lúcio Melo | Sector Chief: Danielle de Souza Guimarães
Responsible Sector: Technical Coordination of Cultural Heritage
Praça Matheus Tavares, 121 | Neighborhood: Center | CEP: 37002-320 | Tel.: (35) 3690-2718

--- seg-586eb7d07fbb4dc4fb6ed481 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"
--- seg-0d7c4b5adf063527590e984f [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | MG
Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-fca9b380f3bd92a8a5cab917 [needs-review] ---
Night and day
The hum, the form, the color
Of a single UFO.)
I well know that they circulate everywhere:
On the beaches
In the finite sky of today
Even at the home of another friend in Teresópolis.
I well know and suffer
From a lack of confidence in this poet
Who has seen many extraterrestrial things
In dreams and awake
He has seen mermaids, dragons
The Prince of Darkness
The incarnate aurora borealis in a woman
The seven archangels of Congonhas da Luz
And sweet souls from the other world in procession.
But the disk, the disk?
It eludes me and laughs at my search.
One flew right by (they say)
Almost brushing against me. Didn't you see? I didn't.
A little person in a rainbow suit (it seems)
Pulled me by the arm: Come on (or: stop),
Maybe...?
This is what my neighbors assure me
And I, called or not called
Insensible and blind without ears
Let my turn pass.
Love, I am a little sad, I am sorrowful
For being the only one
Who has never seen a flying saucer
Now common on Rua do Ouvidor.
Carlos Drummond de Andrade
In: Claro Enigma. José Olympio, 1951

===== PAGE 9 [page-469cf4ec321113d5996aa263] =====
--- seg-dfcc3aa0b6c0f7df171c3149 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"
--- seg-dc3da05bdd32f8a4e5ab601a [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | MG
Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-9d4417a682354a3a4c240bb5 [needs-review] ---
The 1988 Brazilian Federal Constitution, in Article 216, defines Brazilian cultural heritage as "material and immaterial goods, taken individually or collectively, that bear reference to the identity, action, and memory of the different groups that form Brazilian society." Memory, among other attributes, is an important element in the defense of goods that are elected and safeguarded as Brazilian intangible cultural heritage. In the case of the dossier presented here, the good in question pertains to collective memory. Memory is always abstract and relates to a community, a place, a group of people, based on individual memories that are diffused and recreated through social interaction. It is in the collective of experiences and lived memories that they are diffused and can be mythified.

Pierre Nora defined collective memory as "the recollection or set of recollections, conscious or not, of an experienced and/or mythified event by a living community whose sense of the past is an integral part of its identity" (Collective Memory, Dictionary of New History, 1978, p. 398). In the case of the Varginha ET, presented in this dossier, there was a mythification of an experience lived by a limited group of people. This experience, regardless of its nature, was diffused and transformed with great force into one of the identities of an entire municipality, a territory.

The concept of collective memory, as a sociological fact, was first presented by Maurice Halbwachs (2013). For Halbwachs (2013), collective memory is formed in the context of the individual when there is a process of social interaction:
--- seg-c2d430f9d164f27b575fdbc2 [machine-unreviewed] ---
For our memory to benefit from the memories of others, it is not enough for
them to present their testimonies; it is also necessary that it has not diverged
from their memories and that there are many points of contact between one and
the other so that the memory that reminds us is reconstructed on a common base.
It is not enough to reconstruct piece by piece the image of a past event to obtain
a memory. It is necessary that this reconstruction works from common data or
notions that are in our spirit and also in others, because they are always passing
from these to those and vice versa, which will only be possible if they have been
and continue to be part of the same society, the same group (HALBWACHS, 2013,
p. 39).

Although it was Halbwachs who brought the issue of collective memory to the forefront, it was Pierre Nora, with his *Les lieux de mémoire* (1984), that propelled historical studies to debate memory and collective memory. It was in this study that Nora defined the differences between memory and history.
--- seg-23c5e9482dd7438be6187790 [machine-unreviewed] ---
Memory and history: far from being synonyms, we become aware that they are opposites. Memory is life, always carried by living groups, and in this sense, it is in permanent evolution, open to the dialectic of remembrance and forgetting, of the unconsciousness of its successive deformations, vulnerable to all uses and manipulations, susceptible to long latencies and sudden revitalizations. History is the always problematic and incomplete reconstruction of what no longer exists. Memory is a phenomenon always current, a place lived in the eternal present; history, a representation of the past. Because it is affective and magical, memory accommodates itself with details that comfort it; it feeds on vague, telescopic, global or floating memories, particular or symbolic, sensitive to all transfers, scenes, censures or projections. History, as an intellectual and secularizing operation, demands critical analysis and discourse. Memory installs remembrance in the sacred; history liberates it, making it always prosaic. (NORA, 1984, p. XIX).
--- seg-b0b2c799d8c58e49d854cf7b [needs-review] ---
In the case of intangible heritage, although historians and social scientists base studies for the Records on historical issues of goods, it is in the "affective and magical" memory that social groups linked to a particular intangible good are anchored and recreate their practices, collectively and daily. These recreations enable the continuity of the good. In the field of Intangible Heritage, only memories that are alive and latent within a community, that make sense to its holders, and that can be recreated and have continuity in the future, are of interest.

Memory and history are intertwined and both are inseparable, as Paul Ricœur and Jacques Le Goff taught us. There is therefore no competition between memory and history or a classification in terms of superiority or inferiority, but rather a circularity. They can sometimes be antagonistic, sometimes complementary, as modes of managing the past (Villas Boas, 2015).

The UNESCO, for its part, defines "intangible cultural heritage as practices, representations, expressions, knowledge and techniques - together with instruments, objects,

===== PAGE 11 [page-3249394de92e88b0af6243f8] =====
--- seg-065dcbc4dcc851a8442f08ce [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"
--- seg-607838b3151a4983dd337208 [needs-review] ---

--- seg-da76b0a8aded2c51474e07e3 [needs-review] ---
The artifacts and cultural sites associated with them—which communities, groups, and in some cases individuals recognize as an integral part of their cultural heritage—as established in Article 1 of the Convention for the Safeguarding of the Intangible Cultural Heritage, adopted in 2003. According to UNESCO, it is the groups themselves that should recognize a particular asset as their intangible heritage or not.

In the same Convention for the Safeguarding of Intangible Cultural Heritage (2003), it is recognized that intangible heritage is "constantly recreated by communities and groups in response to their environment, their interaction with nature, and their history, generating a sense of identity and continuity and thus contributing to promoting respect for cultural diversity and human creativity." One of the purposes of the Convention for the Safeguarding of Intangible Cultural Heritage, adopted in 2003, stipulates that there should be "respect for the intangible cultural heritage of communities, groups, and individuals involved." And as we have seen, it is the community itself that defines this heritage. It is not within the purview of other social groups or experts in the topics involving Cultural Heritage to judge or define what should be understood as Cultural Heritage of another group.

The Case of the Varginha ET is one of these themes that often inflames positions and suscits opinions and debates—from those who believe in UFO events, to the skeptical, to those who recognize the representations of the case as local heritage, and those who do not. However, the speaking position, in this case, is that of the Varginha community itself, which recognizes the asset as local cultural heritage, as manifested by the signatures on the consents and petitions attached to this Dossier. The hundreds of representations created and recreated daily, which keep the memory of the asset alive, also reinforce its local recognition as a cultural asset.

Considering all the points discussed thus far, this Registration is grounded in both the Brazilian Federal Constitution and the international guidelines of UNESCO, followed by Iphan, which advocate for "respect for the intangible cultural heritage of communities, groups, and individuals involved" and the understanding that it is the communities themselves who should recognize what is or is not their heritage.

The protection derived from this Registration, as strictly described in this Dossier, concerns the representations created in the municipality surrounding the Varginha ET Case. The sense is to safeguard the representations that have been and continue to be produced in the municipality related to the Varginha ET Case.

The representation presented here is based on the definitions of Carlo Ginsburg and Roger Chartier, following UNESCO's understanding that representations, along with practices, expressions, knowledge, and techniques, can be recognized as Intangible Cultural Heritage. Therefore, a new municipal register book, the Register Book of Representations, was opened to inscribe representations, such as the Varginha ET.

Moreover, one of the possibilities for recognizing a cultural heritage asset, both tangible and intangible, is through associations and representations made over time through music, poetry, literature, images, souvenirs, cartoons, statuettes, monuments, buildings, paintings, magazine and newspaper publications, online and offline, among others. The Varginha ET case, as demonstrated in this study, has hundreds of recreations and representations made since 1996 until today. This keeps the asset constantly alive in the collective imagination and offers possibilities for recreation and continuity.

The symbolic values and images associated with an asset and that inspire distinct representations are recognized by UNESCO for the classification of an asset. One of the criteria highlighted by UNESCO for recognizing assets and inscribing them on the Representative Lists of the World Heritage is that the asset is directly or materially associated with living events or traditions, ideas, beliefs, or works of art and literature of exceptional universal significance. For example, in the Rio de Janeiro nomination as a cultural landscape, the associations and representations made of the "marvelous city" were highlighted at various moments in the Dossier.

These associated values should project the culture and relevant aspects of the place represented. When representations of what would be aliens appear in animations, cartoons, or books, having coffee and eating cheese bread, it refers to the local Varginha and Minas Gerais culture as a whole. These representations, indeed, make Varginha recognized through them. If a person is photographed under the replica of the spacecraft installed by COPASA in Varginha's Center, it is not necessary for the person to describe where they are to recognize that they are in Varginha. In other words, the representation, in this case, the simulacrum of the spacecraft, takes the place of the represented thing, which in this case is Varginha and the relation with the ET's history.

===== PAGE 13 [page-7ef8a4d36a2cab132e63f928] =====
--- seg-6a00992c36b16c4f8038096b [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"
--- seg-afddfe98e853e584e49514d0 [needs-review] ---

--- seg-d02cf4aeedf481f3fee5e752 [needs-review] ---
The consistency of representations, speeches, and images created and recreated around the Varginha ET Case kept the memory of the event alive, even for those who never visited Varginha. In the national and global imagination, Varginha is the municipality where the strange creatures allegedly emerged in January 1996, identified by ufologists as extraterrestrial beings. In this sense, the ET is from Varginha, it is in Varginha, even if the supposed appearance event only happened once. It's as if Varginha's territory belongs to the ETs, or at least those who allegedly appeared there. It is very likely that the mystery and secrecy imposed by the authorities, especially the military, around the case fuels imagination, and new representations and narratives are created over time.

Varginha became known worldwide as the city of the Varginha ET from what became known as the "Varginha Incident" or "Varginha Incident" or, alternatively, "Varginha ET Case," as will be referred to in this Dossier.

The event that gave global visibility to the municipality of Varginha, located in the south of the state of Minas Gerais, occurred on January 20, 1996. That day, young Katia Andrade Xavier and sisters Liliane de Fátima Silva and Valquíria Aparecida Silva reportedly saw a creature while crossing a vacant lot between the Jardim Andere and Santana neighborhoods in Varginha. Beside a wall, the three young women spotted a creature with brown, viscous skin as if oil had been applied to it, red-colored large eyes, and a large head with three protuberances resembling horns. At the same time, residents of the surrounding areas of Varginha reported having seen unidentified flying objects (UFOs).

Reports of military and Fire Department movements were granted by several local people who saw the trucks and armed men blocking curious onlookers's passage.

The Varginha ET Case was reported by the Brazilian and international press. Fantástico produced a special report in May 1996, exposing the case (available at: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mItYpoUSJyY) and making it known nationally and internationally. Afterwards, several other journalistic groups visited Varginha to record and report the event.

Starting from the generating event, which at this moment does not matter discussing the truth or not, the municipality of Varginha became known worldwide due to the ET story and space ships. Replicas of the creature described by the three girls began to be produced and large sculptures were installed in the central region of the municipality. Initially, there was a movement from the press and ufology, and later, the population of the municipality started to identify and recreate the story in various representations. Thus, we identify that there was first an outside-in movement regarding the Varginha ET Case, and only later did the municipality itself recognize that narrative. Some citizens, like artisan Mauri Bomfim, created the first representations in large statues and figurines immediately after the event, still in 1996. After him, dozens of people made various representations present in crafts, buildings, urban furniture, sculptures, cartoons, drawings, among others.

With the disclosure and great impact of the case, the municipality "gained" an ET, as in all references about the alien, it appears as the Varginha ET. It is not an ET in Varginha, or an ET that was in Varginha. But it is "of," a personal pronoun. The ET is recognized as being from Varginha, and even cartoons and playful videos refer to an ET who became a miner, wears boots, likes cheese bread, is friendly, and talks with the characteristic accent of the people from the Gerais region. The representations of the ET that would be "of" Varginha appear stereotyped with signs that refer to the people from the interior of Minas Gerais, either by the accent, the clothes, or the taste for coffee and cheese bread.

In the municipality of Varginha, the ET Case is materialized in various furniture, architectural constructions, crafts, illustrations, characters, sculptures, among others.

In 2003, COPASA started building a suspended water reservoir 20 meters above the ground, with a capacity of 100,000 liters of water, in Praça Marechal Floriano, located in the city center, in the shape of an OVNI or space ship. Anyone passing through Varginha, either on vacation or on business, takes a picture next to the "ship," as the construction became known. When the opening of the registration process was disclosed in the media, a person narrated that they had already deviated 90 km from their route on a trip just to take a picture with the "ship."

2. Descriptive analysis of the cultural asset

2.1 History of the cultural asset

2.1 The Formation of South Minas Gerais

South Minas, where the municipality of Varginha was formed, is the Minas Gerais portion bounded by the Grande River and bordering the states of Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo through the Mantiqueira Mountains. It had a late occupation process compared to the central and mining region of the Province of Minas Gerais.

Official history tells of occupation based on the colonial explorer context, but we know that indigenous people inhabited all of Brazil, including South Minas, before the arrival of colonizers and the extermination of indigenous populations.

In the early centuries of occupation, the entire region belonged to the Comarca do Rio das Mortes, with São João del Rei as its seat. This South Minas region was occupied mainly after the discovery of gold in the central Minas region and soon became a target of disputes between the Capitania of São Paulo and the Minas. Geographically, it corresponds to "a vast area intersected by several paths that ensured access and enabled the commercial interconnection of the main slave-holding areas of the South-Central" (ANDRADE, 2005).

Regarding the occupation of South Minas and the arrival of pioneers into the forests and prairies of the Verde River, Lefort (1950) reports:

Since 1737, South Minas has been officially explored. Officially, due to the possessory act of Cipriano José da Rocha, the Ouvidor (judicial officer) of the Comarca de São João del Rei, during his reprisal visit to the quilombos (resistance communities) and the desertion of precious minerals, which he took to São Paulo (LEFORT, 1950).
--- seg-9eb2c8ca973d82539d7ec664 [needs-review] ---
The same author also cites that:

In a private capacity, the mines of Rio Verde have been known and explored by Paulistas for a long time, who refrained from revealing them "to the authorities of São Paulo or Minas. With the execution of the order of the Minas governor, Martinho de Mendonça, a winding trail was torn from São João d'El Rei to the campaigns of Rio Verde, made by the Auditor and his entourage, who spared no effort in breaking through forests and plains that could open the road, which indeed became free for all.

--- seg-71ed951041663911477e90b3 [needs-review] ---
According to Lenharo (1993), the South Minas region constituted the route of troops supplying the Court, and it also became the main production and supply center for the Carioca market. This region was composed of large slave-holding properties essentially focused on internal supply. "The subsistence economy of South Minas would have been established to supply the Province in the 18th century, maintaining the regional economy's nature by directing the surplus to the Rio de Janeiro market" (LENHARO, 1993).

At that time, as Rubião (1919) states, "communications between the interior of Brazil and the coastline were made through trails in the Mantiqueira, on roads branching from the Paraíba Valley, leading to the ports of Rio de Janeiro and Mangaratiba" (RUBIÃO, 1919 apud SALES, 2003, p. 220). These communications, made over long periods, "in small caravans of gold prospectors and sesmeiros, became a source of life and development for the country's interior" (RUBIÃO, 1919 apud SALES, 2003, pp. 220-221).

Rubião continues:

"Troops" organized with mules from Campo Grande, through the emerging fairs of Sorocaba and Taubaté, flooded the interior's roads, bringing salt and iron from the coastline and bringing back hides, cotton fabrics, and other goods. The cities of Campanha and Formiga became commercial centers. The small towns that mediated between the largest centers, planted along the road at that time, grew under the impetus of troop movements, which made trade between these cities and the coastline (RUBIÃO, 1919 apud SALES, 2003, p. 221).
--- seg-18da9e234fa0fd609826d0a9 [needs-review] ---
Andrade (2005) states that the Verde River mines, discovered by Paulistas in the first decades of the 1700s, only became known in 1737 through a military expedition led by the Overseer of the Village of São João del Rei, Cipriano José da Rocha, following orders from the Governor of the Capitany, D. Martinho de Mendonça de Pina e Proença, to survey the region and explore the unknown lands along the Verde River basin.

===== PAGE 19 [page-0fe43cf1dee6241984e9d829] =====
--- seg-8455957069abe4547eb759a9 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"
--- seg-1fedfa9a35eb7d64c83b1094 [needs-review] ---

--- seg-8dc008cb2e144c7f657f3993 [needs-review] ---
Sapucaí and Palmela took possession of the territory in the name of the king. Thus, the settlement of Campanha emerged, and from there, the region was gradually taken over.

Although there were records that the southern region had been visited in 1737, it was, in fact, 52 years later that it became an independent region. The southern region of Minas Gerais was emancipated from the municipality of Campanha, known as Campanha da Princesa, in 1789 (ANDRADE, 2005).

Originally located in the Comarca do Rio das Mortes, which had been emancipated from the central mining region with the establishment of its seat in São João D’El Rey in 1713, Campanha became the seat of the Comarca do Rio Sapucaí in 1833. Naturally, the region that formed the Comarca do Rio Sapucaí had its boundaries delimited to the north by the Rio Grande and to the south and southeast by the Mantiqueira mountain range. Thus, the cradle of Southern Minas is Campanha, a city that would be further divided in the first half of the 19th century into the cities of Baependy (1814), Jacuhy (1814), Pouso Alegre (1831), Lavras (1831), Jaguary (1840), and Itajubá (1848). (...) A regional dynamic that would be affirmed in the first half of the 19th century, embodied in the separatist movements led by the city of Campanha in the last decades of the century. A region whose determinants advance those of the urbanization of the mining region: agriculture and supply trade shape its identity and vocation. (SAES, 2010:14;15)
--- seg-415618f0332f6ea1fbc8547e [needs-review] ---
The Comarca of Rio das Mortes was administratively constituted in the Province of
Minas in 1822, together with three other comarcas: Vila Rica, Serro, and Rio das Velhas. The
name “Rio das Mortes” (River of Death) is due to the comarca's location beside a river with
that name, which had been known since the 17th century.
The three oldest towns in Southern Minas: Aiuruoca, Baependi, and Campanha,
belonged to the ancient Comarca of Rio das Mortes until 1833.
Andrade (2005) states that Campanha is considered the agglomerating core and the origin
of Southern Minas. The parishes of Aiuruoca and Baependi were the first to be established,
in 1718 and 1723, respectively.
The Term of Campanha comprised ten parishes: Lavras do Funil, Baependi,
Pouso Alto, Santa Ana do Sapucaí, Camanducaia, Ouro Fino, Itajubá, Cabo Verde and Jacuí,
Carrancas, and Aiuruoca (ANDRADE, 2005).
In the first half of the 19th century, the region of Southern Minas had commercial
relations with the Empire; at the transition from the 19th to the 20th century, relations
between this region and São Paulo (São Paulo State) were intensified. The South of the State
of Minas Gerais would evolve from a region producing commodities
to an industrial and agricultural power.

The supply of infrastructure for an export-oriented region, thanks to the coffee culture.
It was in the last quarter of the 19th century that the railroad reached the region, along with banks, which only met small needs, as larger businesses were supplied by government sources or banks from Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo. And it was during this period that capitalist commercial practices developed in the region, with the emergence of small factories and the intensification of the commercial sector.
The cities were characterized by being of medium size, with populations between ten and forty thousand inhabitants, but there were also many smaller towns. From 1890, an increase in the number of cities and the expansion of the railway network can be observed. This led to an increase in the region's export capacity to neighboring areas, but also intensified competition from local markets with imported products.
The South Minas region stood out as a coffee producer in the last quarter of the 19th century and throughout the 20th century. However, the same region was also responsible for a traditional agro-pastoral production focused on the domestic market, still at the end of the 18th century and the first half of the 19th century, a condition that contributed to South Minas becoming one of the most dynamic regions of the province/state during the period in question, initially with a significant demographic condition that allowed great political influence between 1870 and 1920.
From these data, we can observe the demographic growth of South Minas cities. Between 1872 and 1920, the region presented the highest population growth rate in the state. In 1920, it was the region with the largest population in Minas Gerais, accounting for 20% of the state's population. In 1972, the region concentrated 260,000 inhabitants, a number that rose to 730,000 in 1907 and finally to approximately 1 million in 1920. Already in the observation of cities, individually, in 1920, none of them (considering them as urban nuclei) had more than 20,000 inhabitants (GAMBI, 2012).
The introduction of coffee in the region at the end of the 19th century would provide the development of new municipalities and the growth of existing ones where the culture developed.
(...) together with the expansion of the population (...) it was clear the growth in the number of cities. Alfenas, Boa Esperança, Três Corações and Varginha, for example, became municipalities where the coffee culture flourished.

part of a first phase of municipal emancipation, between the 1860s and 1880s, as a result of the introduction of coffee in the region (SAES, 2010, p. 19).
--- seg-183a87645c0b31b7c158051d [machine-unreviewed] ---
In the year 1911, the southern municipalities of Minas Gerais would increase, rising from 36 to 48. Thus, the Southern region became more dynamic, expanding its economic activities, compared to other regions of the state. However, of these 48 municipalities, only 5 had a population between seven and twelve thousand inhabitants, 4 had a population between five and seven thousand inhabitants; and 3 had a population between four and five thousand inhabitants (GAMBI, 2012).
--- seg-1973969ac3deae4fadb5d3d6 [needs-review] ---
Unlike the Zona da Mata with Juiz de Fora, the central area from the 1920s with Belo Horizonte and Teófilo Otoni to the Mucuri Valley, the South Minas region would be marked by a deep fragmentation of its population into small and medium-sized cities, without a central irradiating center of economic activities (SAES, 2010, p. 19).
--- seg-a54846951c3f52cde7bfaf16 [needs-review] ---
The railways in southern Minas Gerais were built around 1880, but before that, they had already reached the Mata and the West. They came through four companies: Rio Verde Railway, which later became known as Minas and Rio; Sapucaí Railway; Muzambinho Railway; and the Mogiana Railway Company, which originated in São Paulo and reached the southern region of Minas Gerais in the early 20th century to serve the Triangle and the South. The lack of a central irradiating hub for transportation led, initially, to the integration of southern Minas Gerais' railways with external markets, rather than among the region's own cities.

Although provisionally, it is possible to think that the railways in southern Minas Gerais began to establish themselves to serve a dynamic and promising region focused on the supply trade to Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo, and for coffee planting and export. And more importantly, what is observed is the formation of the railway system with interconnection between southern Minas Gerais and the ports of Rio de Janeiro and Santos, or with the markets of neighboring states, a modernization of transportation that could emerge as a condition to strengthen the internal market, but which, apparently, emerges to tighten relations, including dependencies, with other regions (SAES, 2010, p. 19).
--- seg-eef1d07ce5af2ad7cce465c2 [needs-review] ---
Then the first factories in South Minas Gerais emerged. Compared to those in the Mata region and the metalurgical area, they were more precarious, generally linked to supply or to the municipality.

===== PAGE 22 [page-f684153e38d6c6abdc30ff93] =====
--- seg-b2d164cad1c5ed9d72062fc2 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"
--- seg-9ac69d126f4e4ced90e9f9ac [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | MG
Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-318e4198530258f4911da7ba [needs-review] ---
an agro-export coffee economy with an average of four employees per establishment. In addition, South Minas industries had as a characteristic family-run businesses, such as crafts, and those focused on the food sector, especially for local consumption.

Small and rudimentary manufactures had to compete with imported products from other states or abroad. The company is realized as a family enterprise, archaic, distant from the characteristics of modern large industry. In this sense, South Minas became another specific case within the "mosaic" of Minas: a historically dynamic region, both for its function of supplying the imperial court and for its transformation due to the expansion of coffee plantations in the transition to the 20th century, but which was unable to keep up with the pace and economic vigor of neighboring states, Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo (SAES, 2010, p. 17).
--- seg-3c0c6b1ef0653396652d1a13 [needs-review] ---
In the 1920s, the southern region began expanding its industrial production, becoming second in this sector, as the metallurgical region was first. This was due to the continuity of an archaic industrial structure in metallurgy and the greater representativeness of the southern industry in the Minas Gerais industrial economy. However, even though it had increased its industrial production, revealing greater representativeness in Minas Gerais' industrial production, and was second in the Minas Gerais industrial sector, the southern Minas Gerais industry had not become modern, as there was a lack of a larger consumer market and limited accumulation capacity. Regarding the banks in the region, the development of this sector followed the precarious industrial development: although there was a significant expansion in the number of banks in the south of Minas Gerais between 1910 and 1920, due to the coffee culture and the emergence of railroads and industries, most banking operations were located in external banks in cities like Juiz de Fora (Real Credit Bank) and Belo Horizonte (Mortgage and Agricultural Bank and Commerce and Industry Bank). These three banks were responsible for approximately 90% of the current account deposits in Minas Gerais between 1920 and 1925. The banks in the southern region of Minas were small and intended to serve the local community (COSTA, 1978). Although the development of the southern region of the state, at the turn of the 19th to the 20th century, may even be considered precarious, it was during this time that capitalism in the region began to take root.

===== PAGE 23 [page-8d87eb6092c30c355bbd02b2] =====
--- seg-2cf1ee503d69d65d7b941a8a [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"

--- seg-3905b53a7d376803db6d5875 [needs-review] ---
gaining its form. It is important to highlight that during this transition, only three regions in the state of Minas Gerais underwent a transformation into the capitalist production system: the south, the Mata zone, and the center.

The transformations in the socio-economic relations through which the region passed during the transition from the 19th to the 20th century - the growth and development of cities, coffee cultivation, the emergence of industries, intensification of trade, development of railroads and banks - directed the region towards greater integration with external markets, fostering capitalist production relations.

Among the cities that emerged in the south of Minas Gerais with the expansion of the coffee culture in the region, as the 19th century gave way to the 20th, was Varginha, founded in 1882, initially called Espírito Santo das Catanduvas. In the next item, we will learn about the history of Varginha's formation.
--- seg-a103f8cc8ce6d2a9c9c5b1eb [needs-review] ---
2.2 Formation of Varginha
--- seg-c302dc431e73ea5949fd3e53 [needs-review] ---
The region where the municipality of Varginha is located was inhabited until the end of the 17th century by the Cataguás indigenous group. This ethnic group, whose name means "good people," is described by historians and archaeologists as a warlike and strong people, of medium height, knowledgeable in ceramics, nomadic, living in rudimentary shelters. Catanduvas (or Catandubas), a word of Tupi origin, was the name they gave to this region, meaning "spiny and low forest," common in these lands with clayey and infertile soil.

The Portuguese Crown, aiming to expand and occupy Brazilian territory and seek mineral wealth (stones and precious metals), promoted in the 17th century exploration expeditions into the interior of Brazil - called Entradas or Bandeiras - and as these men, commonly called Bandeirantes, entered the territory, they expelled or exterminated the original inhabitants of the region. From there, the first settlements or arraiais emerged for the supply and rest of the troops, led at various times by the bandeirante Fernão Dias Paes Leme (1608-1681), who would give his name to one of the main highways of Minas Gerais. It was in this context that Varginha emerged.
--- seg-f787f22b19268a795427ac9a [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | Mayor: Verdi Lúcio Melo | Sector Head: Danielle de Souza Guimarães

===== PAGE 24 [page-622101a75df3da25f80bf17b] =====
--- seg-d047026f31dbfb14f5b3fd63 [needs-review] ---

--- seg-cabafaab09be2964696d81ba [needs-review] ---
The first references to Varginha date back to 1763, mentioning the Chapel of Saint Anthony of Rio Verde, located on the old road connecting Três Pontas to the Campaign, likely built by bandeirantes who passed through. Around the chapel, landing ranches for travelers developed, so the settlement grew along the path of troops transported by mules coming from São Paulo, mainly from Sorocaba and
--- seg-8a23af6ebce30afb507e6064 [needs-review] ---
Taubaté, which transported all kinds of goods to the Campaign Village of Princesa da Beira, now the city of Campanha.
The emerging settlement was given the name Catandubas or Catanduvas, which means "Serrated Forest," a common feature in that city. Due to the patron saint of the chapel, it became known as Espírito Santo das Catandubas. In 1806, the village had 1000 people. In the same year, the territory that constitutes the primitive patrimony of the developing nucleus was donated. A year later, the settlement of Espírito Santo was officially established, with a small and modest agricultural village. In 1850, it was elevated to a parish (...) originating the neighborhood of Vargem, located 1 km (0.62 miles) northeast of the village (LEFORT, 1950).
--- seg-9dd048ee42150571a9f61b62 [needs-review] ---
According to Lefort (1950), we should consider the years 1790 to 1793 as the beginning
of the colonization of Varginha. It was at this time that many families from Campanha and
Lavras arrived with their enslaved people and built the first houses on the Santo Antônio farm
of Bom Jardim do Rio Verde, where the Santo Antônio chapel, active since 1763, was located.
The first documented record of the Varginha settlement dates back to 1795 and reports the
birth of Maria Francisca de Jesus, baptized in the Divine Spirit Santo chapel of Catanduvas,
as stated on page 45 of the 5th Book of Lavras from the Campaign M.G Diocese:
"On the third day of April, one thousand seven hundred and ninety-five, at the Divine
Spirit Santo chapel of Catanduvas, a filial of the Matrix of Sant'Ana das Lavras do Funil,
the Reverend Coadjuntor Antônio José dos Santos baptized and anointed with holy oils
Francisca, legitimate daughter of Joaquim Vitoriano de Andrade and Antônia Maria Felícia;
Francisco Nogueira and Maria Francisca served as godparents, of which I have made an
entry that I signed. The vicar, José da Costa Oliveira." (LEFORT, 1950 apud SALES 2003,
pp. 118-119).
--- seg-a5f22231fd6c84d414803bba [needs-review] ---
In the early 19th century, before 1806, the couple Francisco Alves da Silva and Dona Tereza Clara Rosa da Silva acquired the lands where Varginha would later be located. In the year 1806

The couple sold these lands to Ensign Manoel Francisco de Oliveira, and later, these lands were donated to the Diocese of Campanha (SALES, 2003).

At that time, the settlement had approximately 1,000 people. In the same year, the territory that constituted the original patrimony of the developing nucleus was donated. The following year, the Curate of Espírito Santo das Catandubas was established, located in the central region of the contemporary Varginha. For 43 years, the locality was a curate, a sort of village with the necessary conditions to become a district of a municipality. The name Varginha was only documented for the first time in 1816, in the 3rd Book of Marriages of Lavras, a name that had already been popularly used due to the geography of the region, which presents areas of vargem. The main works that marked this period were the constructions of the Matriz Church of Divino Espírito Santo and the Church of Nossa Senhora do Rosário.

Regarding the creation of the Curate of Espírito Santo, Fonseca and Liberal state:
--- seg-7292d2052684c3980ef26fad [needs-review] ---
One year later, on January 18, 1807, the then vicar of Lavras do Funil, Father José da Costa, reported to the General Vicar the fulfillment of the order, thus creating the Curacy of Espírito Santo, the embryo of the future and rich city that would later be called Varginha. (...) In the status of a curacy, under ecclesiastical jurisdiction, the locality remained for 44 years, with a small and modest agricultural life, trading with the nearest cities, until it was elevated to the category of parish, annexed to the neighboring municipality of Três Pontas, by Law No. 471, Article 1, Paragraph 1, of June 1, 1850 (Fonseca & Liberal, 1919, as cited in Sales 2003, pp. 202-203).
--- seg-80ebb960b8c08c040de839ae [needs-review] ---
In 1820, Varginha (still known as Espírito Santo das Catanduvas) had "6 houses with tiles and some thatched huts, all built near the chapel, at the site where today the Rio Branco Avenue is" (Rubião, 1919 apud Sales, 2003, p. 219). By 1870, Varginha had 213 houses. Sales (2003) states that between 1850 and 1881, the first public service buildings, such as school buildings, were constructed. In 188, Varginha had 300 buildings, some of which were two-story.

On June 1st, 1850, the parish was elevated to a municipality through Provincial Law No. 471, being annexed to the municipality of Três Pontas. Varginha would then experience its first development surge. The first public buildings were constructed, such as the first two public schools and the jail.

Espírito Santo das Catanduvas would be transformed into the municipality of Espírito Santo da Varginha on September 22, 1881, by Law No. 2785. A municipality can be defined as an administrative division of a region, with administrative autonomy, possessing a City Hall and a Municipal Council.

On October 7, 1882, by Law No. 2950, the village of Espírito Santo da Varginha was elevated to a city. Regarding the creation of the municipality, Fonseca & Liberal (1920) report:

--- seg-fa4b0c188f4b4265cb5e8abb [machine-unreviewed] ---
Regarding the formation and urban evolution of Varginha, Ávila (1983) clarifies:
--- seg-f5deef16ae2d555a3ae176b3 [needs-review] ---
Varginha adhered, in its early stages of administrative autonomy and economic affirmation characterized by the period between 1882 and 1892, to the standards still prevalent in the Minas Gerais cities of the time. The road network developed parallel or perpendicular to the old main paths of the primitive village, resulting in a haphazard or circumstantial layout, in a process of expansion conditioned by the urgency or imposition of new constructions to house the growing population. The main trunk road, along whose margin the first chapel appeared in the 1870s and the first six tiled houses were built between that year and 1820, corresponded to the road or simple dirt path connecting the Campaign Village to the then-called Three Points Backlands, and it is presumed to have cut diagonally through the current central perimeter of the city. This initial cluster of habitations formed in a location near where, much later, the Rio Branco Avenue (ÁVILA, 1983, apud SALES, 2003, pp. 224-225) would open.
--- seg-6c7fc63cd2b6f49e8c06d948 [needs-review] ---
Ávila (1983) conducts an analysis of contrast between the large urban voids created by Rio Branco Avenue and public squares and the denser core of the side streets:
--- seg-946316ae5553974e0abf6207 [needs-review] ---
It is worth emphasizing that it has always been a constant, throughout the urban history of the former Catanduvas village, the contrast between larger or more extensive vacancies and narrow circulation ways or dense residential concentration, vacancies almost always intended for the construction of more prominent buildings, with community functions as well as festive events or leisure activities (...) the city, in reality, functioned as a kind of grand noble courtyard or veranda extending the dozens of farms that surrounded it, at a close, medium or relative distance, an urbanized territory where rural owners and eventually their aggregates traded their products, stocked their depots or pantries, communicated like the national poles of the time - São Paulo and Rio de Janeiro -, promoted the preparatory education of their children and participated in acts, events and rituals of political, social and religious conviviality. (ÁVILA, 1983 apud SALES, 2003, p. 225).
--- seg-1538d57168a4f07d85c773e8 [needs-review] ---
The first more evident manifestation of urban sectorization in Varginha, according to Ávila (1983), "is the one that begins to take shape around 1892, with the arrival of the railroad and the inauguration of the railway station, determining not only the expansion of the city into the land strips bordering the railroad, but also the concentration of municipal activities there.

===== PAGE 28 [page-fead9503be2cabb35ff4ccc0] =====
--- seg-c947c7be8ea9055fc259f67e [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"

"more favored from a new means of transport and communication" (ÁVILA, 1983 apud SALES, 2003, p. 225). Further on, the author continues:

"It is therefore observed, under the influence of the advent of the railroad and, simultaneously, the growing economic expression of coffee, the clear definition, within the expanding urban framework, of a special sector directed (...) towards commercial and industrial location" (ÁVILA, 1983 apud SALES, 2003, p. 226).
--- seg-cfd52b5644571d5468cdfd5d [needs-review] ---
In this context, according to Ávila (1983), it was possible to observe the social hierarchy of Varginha's urban space, "as in the oldest and central part of the city, high-income residents, such as merchants, farmers, and service providers, predominated, while the emerging and lower-income population concentrated in the area adjacent to or beyond the railroad" (ÁVILA, 1983, cited in SALES, 2003, p. 227). Currently, this segregation of urban social space is evident.

In 1890, the first Varginha newspaper, the Gazeta de Varginha, was inaugurated. In 1892, the railroad reached the city: it was the Muzambinho Railroad, which arrived in the municipality with financial investments from the first mayor, Matheus Tavares da Silva. In 1892, the urban area had 13 streets, 6 squares, 2 public buildings (one for municipal functions, like the jail) and 3 churches (Matriz, São Sebastião, and Rosário). In 1899, Varginha was experiencing a growing number of houses, and thus, the city began to pave its streets.

In 1902, the public lighting system was inaugurated, using acetylene gas. In 1913, the Varginha Telephone Company was founded, with over 300 installed phones, and the Municipal Cemetery was relocated from Praça da Fonte, along with the demolition of the old Rosário Church. Electric lighting was installed in 1914, and the Municipal Market was opened in 1915. In the same year, the first bank in the city, the Banco Hypotecário Agrícola, was established on Avenida Rio Branco, followed in 1918 by the Banco do Brasil, located at Praça Dom Pedro II. These events demonstrate the city's significant dynamization towards modernization.

The establishment of a newspaper, the arrival of the railroad, the expansion of streets and houses, as well as the creation of cultural and educational institutions, are facts that illustrate the municipality's progress in its economic and social development. In 1918, the city had a population of [...]

2,200 houses and approximately 400 buildings. In that year, the Marista College established itself in the city. In the 1920s, the Commerce Hotel was built. In 1923, the Saints Angels College was founded by three religious women, and in 1924, the Afonso Pena State Group, the first public school in the municipality, supported by the state, emerged. In 1927, the Capitólio Theater was inaugurated, designed by the industrialist José Navarra. Of eclectic style, it was built by the brothers Antônio and Celestino Pires, whose plan, designed by engineer Frisoti Agostini, was modified at the request of the company. The facade and interior decoration are attributed to the Italian Alexandre Valatti. The theater's name was chosen through a popular vote.

During its administrative development, Varginha belonged to the Rio das Mortes Comarca, a vast territory with imprecisely defined boundaries. It also briefly belonged to Lavras and later to the Três Pontas Comarca. Varginha also incorporated other districts: Elói Mendes, emancipated in 1911, and Carmo da Cachoeira, emancipated in 1938. The municipality, at the end of the 19th century and the beginning of the 20th century, had elements that can be considered relevant when it comes to the process of capitalist production mode development in a region. Below, we will discuss a bit about the importance of these factors in the formation of the Varginha municipality in the context of southern Minas Gerais.
--- seg-68c0bc051d8347b147196d79 [needs-review] ---
2.3 The municipality of Varginha: aspects of development in the 19th century and early 20th century
--- seg-df1325081e91cce1cf6eeddc [needs-review] ---
Compared to other much older municipalities with stronger and developed economies like Campanha, Varginha experienced its golden age, according to Sales (2003), between 1881 and 1913. Marked by social and economic development, Varginha became a regional expression center from the creation of the municipality and the city in 1881 and 1882, the abolition in 1888, the elevation to the seat of a comarca in 1890, the implementation of the railroad in 1892, and Italian immigration in the late 1800s and early 1900s, in effect determined Varginha's progress throughout the 1900s.

Initially, the municipality's economy was composed of commercial establishments, small industries, and banks. Varginha sent tobacco, bacon, pigs, and cattle to the court, receiving there ironworks, beverages, and salt. In 1907, the city exported coffee and dairy products.

===== PAGE 30 [page-95cdf246cecd46593bb86c66] =====
--- seg-ec96fd0b534caad4f3382034 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"
--- seg-64bb246c83f60ab397827934 [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | MG
Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-c469ca368a10ab26cef12085 [needs-review] ---
In 1917, coffee became the main export product, alongside livestock. Wheat, salt, and textiles were the main imported goods. Following the decline of the mining economy, the coffee economy allowed Brazil to make significant strides toward consolidating capitalist production relations, marking the beginning of Brazilian industrial development. It was in the states of São Paulo and Rio de Janeiro that capitalism developed most rapidly due to the conditions coffee provided to these regions.

Since 1870, the city of São Paulo has experienced intense economic growth, which, thanks to the support of the coffee culture, led to the expansion of activities related to the formation of industries, railroads, and banks. These activities, which were an extension of the coffee industry, developed because in São Paulo, coffee created an economic complex in the region, meaning that these other activities—banks, industries, trade, and railroads—arose because of coffee, to serve the coffee economy.

By the late 19th century, the Southern region of Minas Gerais also received influences from the expansion of the coffee economy, stimulating urban growth in the area. Coffee was also responsible for the economic transformations that occurred in Minas Gerais, with particular emphasis on the Zona da Mata. However, the Southern region of Minas Gerais also benefited from the advantages brought by coffee cultivation.

The dynamics generated by the coffee economy allowed railroads to develop in the South, starting in the third quarter of the 19th century, which facilitated the transport of goods and increased the profits generated by coffee. Not only were railroads an extension of the coffee economy in the Southern region of Minas, but also industries (starting in 1889), trade, and the banking network (primarily from the 20th century).

Coffee cultivation in the Southern region of Minas expanded to the municipalities of Aiuruoca, Jacuí, and Baependi in the Sapucaí River Valley by the late 18th century (FILETO; ALENCAR, 2001). In the second half of the 19th century, coffee gained more space to the North and West, reaching Lavras, Nepomuceno, Perdões, Bom Sucesso, and Campo Belo. To the East, coffee plantations advanced into the locations of Monte Santo de Minas, São João da Fortaleza (now Arceburgo), Santa Bárbara das Canoas (Guaranésia), and Cabo Verde. All established as an extension of the expansion of Western São Paulo (OLIVEIRA; GRIMBERG, 2007).

===== PAGE 31 [page-c355ce056b850beb84c6e31f] =====
--- seg-20c1919b36ec278602db9859 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"
--- seg-d4529d84cbe0d8141f0c3e2b [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | MG
Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-f9f846dc3e724a13af1cec33 [needs-review] ---
Throughout the second half of the 19th century, coffee cultivation began to leave the Paraíba Valley and expand into the old western regions of São Paulo (Campinas) and, a few years later, into the new western regions (Ribeirão Preto) with large plantations. There were vast areas of fertile land and good topography. In the province of São Paulo, slave labor was being replaced by European immigration, primarily from Italy, due to the Law of Free Womb and constant abolitionist movements. In the same context, the rapid expansion of the railway network greatly facilitated the disposal of productions (SEMEGHINI, 1991).

Regarding slave labor, Lefort (1950) states that in 1871, Minas Gerais had 340,000 enslaved people. Of these, 1,427 lived in Varginha, with 725 men and 702 women.

For Rubião (1919), the year 1888, the date of the liberation of the captives, found the municipality of Varginha in the most complex period of its economic development. According to the author, "the transformation of the old sugar cane cultivation that was then carried out on a large scale in the municipality of Varginha, to the detriment of our forests brutally sacrificed in the furnaces of the mills, into the new coffee cultivation that required a chosen and numerous workforce, was taking place slowly in an atmosphere of doubts and uncertainties" (RUBIÃO, 1919 apud SALES, 2003, p. 166).

On the eve of the abolition of slavery, the economic, political, and social elite of Varginha was tense and insecure about the fate of their most valuable and lucrative assets. In this sense, a resolution of the Municipal Council, at a session on April 20, 1888, proposes an appeal to the farmers of the municipality to gather with the purpose of discussing Varginha's economic future:

The President proposed that, in view of the abnormal state of relations between slaves and their masters and fearing a disaster for the cultivation due to the immigration of slaves to points where they are more protected in the quest for freedom, this Council address an appeal to all farmers and slaveholders of the municipality to gather in this city on May 13 of this year to discuss the transition from servile to free labor, and that this appeal be published in the press (ATA DA CÂMARA MUNICIPAL DE VARGINHA, 1888).
--- seg-6509a3fd3e1f7c2b40568c08 [needs-review] ---

===== PAGE 32 [page-454756e4fad848f83e33b997] =====
--- seg-fe34b987661b91d1a33d8311 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"
--- seg-62285ef53d10bfb2731f9b05 [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | MG
Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-9b05ca43052e95cc89bf342e [needs-review] ---
The president of the Chamber, whose role was equivalent to that of a mayor, Major Evaristo Gomes de Paiva, was aware of the turbulent situation unfolding in the city, a result of abolitionist struggles followed by the same agitation that plagued Brazil as a whole. The atypical relationships between enslaved people and their owners revealed the desire of the former to break free from their chains. In the face of the inevitable, the local elite gathered to discuss measures necessary to transition from servile to free labor with minimal damage to the municipal economy and, consequently, to their own.

Due to the understanding of the imminent end of slavery, farmers, through the Municipal Chamber that actually represented them, sought to replace slave labor with European immigration, primarily bringing Italians to the Varginha plantations. This action was a purely pragmatic measure, based on an exclusively economic paradigm.

In the final decades of slavery, the number of captives in Varginha was significant, organized primarily in the plantations as mentioned earlier. However, even before the abolition, many farmers in the municipality had already freed their slaves, as in some farms in the southern region of Minas (SALES, 2003, p. 168). The fact that the meeting was scheduled ten days before the end of the institution of slavery demonstrates the attachment to the servile institution and the desperation of the economic elite regarding the loss of their productive goods. Nevertheless, after the abolition, there is no mention in the minutes of any reference to the situation of the newly freed black people in the city of Varginha.

According to Luiz José Álvares Rubião, a memorialist from the early 1900s, the transition from sugar cane to coffee, which the Varginha plantations underwent, requiring intense and numerous labor, occurred slowly amidst a climate of doubt and uncertainty. Thus, new labor was needed to take over the tasks of the slaves, who were already scarce in the city. He argues that Italian immigration saved the plantation and the economy of Varginha.

Sales (2003) highlights that the black race had a decisive influence on the development of Varginha, especially with the introduction of coffee farming before the onset of Italian immigration (SALES, 2003, p. 167).

===== PAGE 33 [page-01dddf963ee471918a325a8c] =====
--- seg-129db4ecf023e9ece321e3a4 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"

--- seg-a95296bf38c1c59701a70865 [needs-review] ---
Indeed, the influence of enslaved and later freed people on the economic development of Varginha, now an important southern Minas Gerais agricultural and industrial center, was decisive, particularly in the trade and export of coffee to other countries.

Varginha, like the city of São Paulo, experienced economic growth and coffee production, and relied on Italian immigration to achieve progress in the late 19th century. From 1888, the wave of immigration that brought foreigners, primarily from Italy, to Brazil reached Varginha, stemming from São Paulo. The Italians then took over coffee plantations, becoming merchants, landowners, industrialists, etc., over time.

The southern Minas Gerais region was rich in fertile, inexpensive land, and became sought after as suitable terrain for establishing coffee farms.

According to Oliveira and Grinberg:

(...) in some cases, they were farming families simply seeking new lands. In others, they were relatives of owners of commission houses or bank houses. As the lands were cheap, some Italian immigrants who originally worked as farmers, after saving some capital and relying on relatives, became landowners. Other farmers started as drovers or carters, transporting coffee sacks on donkeys.

Later, they invested in land, became producers, but some did not stop trading, precisely to finance their own agricultural activity. Similarly, employees of commission houses, tasked with buying coffee directly from farmers, saved and invested in land, becoming producers. (OLIVEIRA; GRINBERG, 2007, p. 20).
--- seg-25a99049c25b01c22e1b07af [needs-review] ---
Therefore, abundant and low-cost fertile lands, idle labor force, expansion of railway lines, and most importantly, a long period of attractive coffee prices, explain the rapid expansion of coffee cultivation in South Minas.
Pires (2004) highlights the importance of the development of South Minas coffee cultivation and its relevance compared to the production of the Zona da Mata, which combined give us the total Minas production:
In the 1970s, Minas production was second in the country. Its exports represented 27% of the national total. Until that time, almost all the coffee exported was produced in the Zona da Mata. From the late 1980s, coffee cultivation intensified in South Minas (...); with this, the weight of the South Minas product increased in the total export calculation. Until the end of the Empire, most of the exported coffee came from the Zona da Mata.

--- seg-d3e6de9c55538f30ec61f660 [needs-review] ---
Coffee brought with it electricity, the automobile, and the telephone, fine fabrics, paved streets, and the construction of theaters and other "novelties." Therefore, the economy of southeastern Brazil at the end of the 19th century was marked by the coffee expansion, accompanied by a progressive ideology modeled after France that would determine the paths of progress and civilization. Varginha was not exempt from this process, especially when coffee began to expand in the southern Minas Gerais region and slowly became its main product at the beginning of the 20th century.

It was during the expansion of the coffee economy in southern Minas Gerais that the city of Varginha developed, initially called Espírito Santo da Varginha, having been emancipated from Três Pontas in 1881.

The main economic activities in Varginha were trade and coffee cultivation. Coffee cultivation was introduced in the municipality around 1870 (RUBIÃO, 1919 apud SALES, 2003), but gained greater economic prominence from the 20th century onwards, and it was common to hear praise for the development of the culture in Varginha's soil, as seen below:
--- seg-fbaf787030defe658dce229d [needs-review] ---
The municipality's lands are fertile and suitable for any crop. (...) Varginha municipality is the true Eldorado of coffee in the southern region of Minas, this precious ruby-colored crop being very well known and appreciated in all markets - national and foreign - for its excellent quality that is not found in any part (CAPRI, 1918 apud SALES, 2003:284).
--- seg-c21e3b2468ced8f384d8befb [needs-review] ---
Regarding the rise of culture, Capri stated that:

"Coffee constitutes the main source of wealth for the municipality. After (...) the Mata Zone, Varginha is the municipality in the state of Minas that exports the most coffee, with the difference that due to its quality, 'Varginha' coffee is considered unique and exceptional (...) . The level of development that the coffee crop has reached in this prosperous municipality is due to the prodigious fertility of the land, whose more homogeneous and resistant to the life of the coffee plant chemical composition, makes it even advantage the coffee land par excellence, which is São Paulo." (CAPRI, 1918 apud SALES, 2003:284)

With the generalized decline of local non-coffee exports in the 1880s, reflecting the replacement by coffee production and the emergence of new outflow routes. Indeed, the expansion of the railways would radically alter the features of the coffee economy and its structuring within the national territory. In the country, with the valorization of lands near railroads, the decline of cities and more remote regions, the imposition of monoculture, and the promotion of urbanization, among other notable aspects in the process (SEMEGHINI, 1991).

Thus, the arrival of the railroad represented a true transformation in the economy of South Minas. In the production process, it allowed an increase in profit margins by reducing freight costs and eliminating losses. The railroad also facilitated the displacement of labor, an essential requirement in the formation of the labor market.

The emergence of the railroad in Varginha, at the end of the 19th century, was a very important milestone for the economic development of the municipality and the southern region of Minas Gerais.

The railroad reached the city in 1892. Thus, the distances between Varginha and other municipalities would be traveled faster, thanks to the modernity of the time, which counted on financial investments from Matheus Tavares da Silva for its inauguration. The then first president of the City Council and owner of the greatest wealth of Varginha, during the period of 1882 to 1883, a merchant, trader, owner of large extensions of land, and holder of many slaves7, died in 1905, leaving two sons and five grandchildren. Regarding the railroad, Sales (2003) states:
--- seg-67d016c28114e1351257f00b [needs-review] ---
It is undeniable that the passage of the railway through Varginha greatly contributed to the development of the city and the region. However, the news of the inauguration received only a small note, untitled, in Minas Gerais, the official organ of the State's powers, in the 'Various News' column, on May 30, 1892: "The Varginha Station of the Muzambinho Company was inaugurated" (SALES, 2003:180).
--- seg-68968fecb65b571f472ee792 [needs-review] ---
At the end of the 19th century and beginning of the 20th century, the process of Italian immigration strengthened the economy of the municipality, leading to closer working relationships with capitalist production relations. Varginha was one of the municipalities that received the most Italian immigration in Brazil during this period.

The memorialist Rubião (1919) reports that Italian immigration saved the coffee crop, as it was not possible to rely on the labor of recently freed blacks, local farmers then hired the first families of Italian immigrants for their farms. Lefort (1950) points to the importance and influence of Italian immigration in the municipality:

One of these migratory currents in 1888 that settled in Varginha was the Italian. Branching out from São Paulo and augmented by new elements from the old world.

Here they embraced culture, their original specialty, supplying the country's markets with the precious rubiaceous plant. After beginning their lives in agriculture, many of the Italian workers eventually became capitalists, merchants, strongmen, wealthy landowners, active industrialists, contributing effectively to the uplift of the municipality's natural wealth. Varginha owes its economic impetus and a great part of its progress, whether in industry, commerce, or liberal professions, to them. (LEFORT, 1950 apud SALES, 2003:174).
--- seg-e24db8dbc5925e91dccbc08b [needs-review] ---
Another significant flow was that of Syrian-Lebanese immigrants, which occurred in the early 20th century, with these immigrants mostly engaged in the trade of products from the capital of São Paulo. Immigration in Varginha was dominated by Italians, but there were also waves of Portuguese, Spanish, German, Argentine, and, in smaller numbers, Asian immigrants. According to Sales (2003), Italian immigrants initiated a process of miscegenation with the Varginha population, leading to profound changes in local habits and culture.

Coffee, a product of immense importance to the municipality's economy, was directly linked to industry. "Among the main industrial establishments, those dedicated to the processing and re-processing of coffee stand out, all located in proximity to Varginha's railway station" (Rubião, 1919, as cited in Sales, 2003, p. 280). Other notable producers included those of: brandy, sugar cane, butter, and mechanical workshops.

Another extremely important factor for the progress of any region is the development of banking institutions. "As elements propelling the vast trade of Varginha, which radiates over a large area of the southern part of the state, thus rightfully earning them the commercial primacy, the city counts on two magnificent establishments" (Fonseca & Liberal, 1920, as cited in Sales, 2003, p. 281).

In 1915, a branch of the State of Minas Gerais' Hypotecary and Agricultural Bank was opened in the city, as was a branch of the Bank of Brazil. The growing needs of our agriculture, the constant progress of our trade, and Varginha's prominent position as a coffee-producing and commercial center in Southern Minas Gerais, had long required a credit establishment in this region. It is remarkable how this city, with an enormous number of captains, a growing record of savings, and a continuous flow of transactions, could sustain an entire massive circulation of credit without a bank. (RUBIÃO, 1919, quoted in SALES, 2003:281;282).

We also observed some characteristics of the shops, the first factories, the arrival of the railroad and banks in the municipality. The local agricultural elite integrated part of the municipality's progress, along with the working class. Farmers, coffee planters and merchants contributed to Varginha making its history.

2.4 Varginha: Economic Aspects (1930 - 2012)
--- seg-ba72607933a5d32f7b0ceced [needs-review] ---
Varginha boasts a privileged and strategic location, situated on the shores of Lake Furnas, and at the same time equidistant from three major Brazilian capitals: São Paulo, Rio de Janeiro, and Belo Horizonte.

Agricultural activities, historically established in the municipality, are most prominent in coffee farming, which serves as the driving force of the local economy. The municipality is home to various warehouses, farmer cooperatives, and other forms of business organization, emphasizing Varginha's role in the national economy.

Given its geographical position and ease of acquiring raw materials and disposing of production, the municipality hosts industries in various sectors, with a focus on agricultural product processing and transformation industries, including the presence of multinational companies. The income generated by agriculture and industry fuels the local economy, encouraging investment in diverse commercial and service activities.

Varginha Municipality | Mayor: Verdi Lúcio Melo | Sector Head: Danielle de Souza Guimarães
Responsible Sector: Technical Coordination of Cultural Heritage

===== PAGE 39 [page-9f4e46204096fe432acff9df] =====
--- seg-fb426c857ee1083bfedbdbe6 [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"
--- seg-18dba5e9326b499fb201e158 [needs-review] ---
Varginha Municipality | MG
Year 2023 | Exercise 2025
--- seg-dba6cadde5f70b0e6954bd10 [needs-review] ---
Brazilian society experienced a significant process of socio-cultural and economic transformations in the 20th century, transitioning from an agrarian society to one with increasingly industrial and urban characteristics. (MARTINE et al., 1989; MARTINE, 1994).

Since the 1930s, with the decline of the coffee monoculture agro-export model and the country's rapid urbanization due to industrialization, the relationships between rural and urban areas have been intensely transformed by the dynamics of capitalist production conditions.

The Great Depression (1929-1933) caused substantial losses for the coffee industry and, consequently, for the entire Brazilian economy (combined with the issue of overproduction), as it became subject to external market conditions, leading to an awareness of Brazil's dependence on coffee policy, which was largely focused on the international market, or the vulnerability of an agro-export economy.

At the onset of the crisis, there was a stagnation of the economy across all sectors, but from 1933, the economy began to grow again; primarily the industrial sector, agriculture recovered slowly. And with the consolidation of industries, the national society started consuming internally produced goods, replacing imports. This led many coffee growers to abandon their large plantations and invest in other sectors, mainly industries. Many of these farmers changed their course because the coffee market was very vulnerable.

However, the 1930s was a crucial period for Brazil's development, as what existed before were small factories, and it was only in the 1930s that the process of Brazilian industrialization began.

In this context, consumer markets shrunk dramatically. In Varginha, things were no different. In the face of the crisis, coffee growers, as usual, sought support from the federal government, but it was unable to continue the protectionist policy for the sector.

Indeed, the 1929 crisis was also a significant factor in weakening the political power of the coffee oligarchies, and it made it clear to the dominant elites that the agro-export model of economy was not viable and had limitations.

Over time, the coffee industry (processing and export) surpassed production (farming) in the city.
Varginha begins to expand. New neighborhoods emerge. The first one, Vila Barcelona, was mostly inhabited by workers.
In the 1950s, according to estimates by the State Department of Statistics of Minas Gerais, coffee production represented 85.84% of the municipality's total agricultural production. Livestock farming was also well-developed, particularly bovine livestock which occupied 76.84%, in terms of herd value. And industry played a prominent role in the municipality's economic life, mainly manufacturing and fabric industry, with 73.88% of the employed capital. It was therefore found that the base of Varginha's economy was agriculture, followed by manufacturing and fabric industry.
At that time, the city was still restricted to the "core" of the center. Houses ended at Avenida Major Venâncio, "Areião" (Fátima), Vila Barcelona and Três Bicas. Neighborhoods like Catanduvas, Jardim Andere, Bom Pastor did not exist, and were considered rural areas.
The industrial evolution Varginha undergoes only begins significantly from mid-1950s with the installation of CBC Indústrias Pesadas S.A., initially named "Companhia Brasileira de Caldeiras" and "Companhia Brasileira de Caldeiras e Equipamentos Pesados", respectively, founded in September 1955, marking the industrialization of Varginha, though modest by the end of the 1950s. The company came to the city before Varginha had institutions to train workers, whose implementation became more notable in the 1960s and 1970s. It was the municipality's largest company, employing up to 3,000 people.
Regional branches of state and federal governments begin to be created. The era of higher education starts in 1965, with the first higher education school (Faculdade de Filosofia, Ciências e Letras). In the following years, Faculdade de Direito de Varginha, Faculdade de Ciências Contábeis e de Administração, Faculdade de Engenharia Mecânica (now defunct Fenva) and Fepesmig, which later became Centro Universitário do Sul de Minas, are established. More recently, Unifenas and Unifal.

===== PAGE 41 [page-628177d83dc2440b2e553177] =====
--- seg-6919b251c7788f7a0be7536f [needs-review] ---
Registration Process of immaterial good: "The E.T. Case and the Vargas Identity"

--- seg-cf7f37b5854736b0ab6c74af [needs-review] ---
Starting from the 1970s, the process of industrialization of the municipality intensified and modernization in coffee production took place. Varginha's agricultural vocation was gradually replaced by industry and service provision. The same occurred with coffee, with roasting and export numbers more significant than the municipality's crop, generating income for the city. This adaptation to the market and new times allowed Varginha to become the second coffee trading square in the world, only losing to Santos, on the coast of São Paulo.

Varginha's contemporary industrial park began to take shape with companies like Moinho Sul Mineiro, Café Bom Dia, Polo Film, Plavigor, FL Smidth, Heatmaster, CBC, and Cooper Standard.

In the 1990s, the growth of the southern and southwestern regions of Minas Gerais led to the installation, in Varginha in 1993, of the Interior Customs Station or EADI, more commonly known as the South Minas Dry Port, being the first to enter operation in Brazil. The stations were created in the 1990s by the Federal Revenue Secretariat with the aim of easing the flow of goods in ports, airports, and border points across the country. This resulted in faster clearance of goods as customs procedures are carried out near the establishments of importers/exporters.

According to the Federal Revenue, in 2012, Varginha's Dry Port exported approximately $119.641 million in goods.

Currently, the municipality is one of the main centers of coffee trade, export, and production in Brazil. According to the IBGE (2010), Varginha's economy has as its main sectors services and industry. And still according to the institute, in 2012, the municipality had the highest GDP in the South Minas Region, around R$ 4,007,089,000.00, and one of the highest in the state. The tertiary sector accounts for 53.2% of the total economically active population, the secondary sector accounts for 34.3%, and the primary sector 12.5%. The municipality has a notable industrial park and a health network, being the main public service hub in the South Minas Region.
